## Output

```
**1. Honest Or Artifact**
Mostly honest, with a routing caveat.

The E-modal result is not mainly an artifact if the dominant driver is simultaneous high sugar and high saturated fat. Those are category-agnostic public-health signals, not quirks of cracker/snack-bar scoring. If most products cross both Israeli red-label thresholds, an E-heavy shelf is a defensible finding.

The artifact risk is not “these should be B/C but got punished.” The artifact risk is interpretive: scoring cookies through `cracker`, `snack-bar`, or `bread` lenses may make the rationale look incoherent or expose irrelevant sub-rules. But the core outcome, hard punishment for sugar plus saturated fat, is directionally credible.

**2. Routing Problem**
Yes, routing matters for governance and explainability, but probably not enough to justify lifting scores.

A dedicated cookie/biscuit category is useful if it does three things:

1. Prevents semantically wrong routing.
2. Makes the comparison page explainable.
3. Preserves the two-red-label cap unless evidence says otherwise.

It should not be added to “normalize” cookies upward. That would be special pleading. If a cookie category produces materially higher grades despite the same sugar and saturated-fat profile, that needs a strong written justification. Otherwise it is grade inflation.

Best test: reroute the 61 items into a cookie category that only removes irrelevant category assumptions, while keeping red-label caps intact. If the distribution stays E/D-heavy, routing was not the main cause.

**3. Endemic Relief**
No broad endemic relief.

Your earlier distinction still holds: cookie sugar plus fat is largely a formulation choice, unlike brine sodium in brined cheese. Cookies can vary in sugar, fat source, saturated fat, fiber, whole grain content, portioning, and processing. The fact that the shelf is indulgent does not make the harms structurally unavoidable.

A bounded recalibration could be legitimate only as a category ceiling rule, not as relief from the signal. Example: “coffee biscuits cannot exceed C/B because this is an indulgence shelf, but within that ceiling we rank better formulations.” That is different from softening the two-red-label cap. I would not weaken the cap unless you have evidence that the current cap destroys useful rank ordering or misclassifies clearly better formulations.

**4. Honest Page Framing**
Yes, the page is useful if framed as “least-bad,” not “healthy cookies.”

A C-ceiling page can work well if the promise is explicit:

“Most coffee biscuits are high in sugar and saturated fat. None are strong nutritional choices. If you buy them anyway, these are the less-bad options by Bari’s scoring.”

Avoid demoralizing language. Make the utility practical:

1. “No A/B products found.”
2. “Top options are C, not health foods.”
3. “Main shelf problem: sugar plus saturated fat.”
4. “Use this page to reduce harm within an indulgence category.”

That is more trustworthy than forcing a cheerful ranking where the category does not deserve one.

**5. My Call**
Ship E-modal as the honest finding. Add a dedicated `cookie/biscuit` routing category only to fix taxonomy and explainability, not to raise grades. Keep the two-red-label cap intact unless a rerouting-only experiment proves the current engine is applying unrelated penalties that materially distort rank order beyond the cap. Do not add D7 endemic relief now; sugar plus saturated fat in cookies is not structurally required in the same way brine sodium is. The right product decision is a sober page: “this is an indulgence shelf, the ceiling is C, and these are the least-bad choices.”

    "If proposing D7: require evidence registry reference, label observability, category scope, rollback plan, and rule-accumulation review before approval."

}
